INTRODUCTION
 
            I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Home Affairs having been authorised by the Committee to submit the Report on its behalf, do hereby present this Seventy-seventh Report of the Committee on the Indian Divorce (Amendment) Bill, 2000*.
2.         In pursuance of the Rules relating to the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committees, the Chairman, Rajya Sabha in consultation with the Speaker, Lok Sabha referred ** the Indian Divorce (Amendment) Bill, 2000, as introduced in the Rajya Sabha on December 15, 2000 and pending therein, to the Committee for examination and report.
 
3.0       The Committee considered the Bill in four sittings held on 23 January, 9 February, 29 May and 13 June, 2001.
 
3.1       The Committee took oral evidence of the Secretary, Legislative Department, Ministry of Law, Justice and Company Affairs in its sitting held on 23 January 2001.
 
3.2       In its sitting held on 9 February, 2001, the Committee heard Secretary General of Catholic Bishops’ Conference of India, Representatives of National Council of Churches in India and Director, Joint Women’s Programme on the various provisions of the Bill.
 
3.3       In its sitting held on 29 May 2001, the Committee took up clause-by-clause consideration of the Bill.
 
3.4       The Committee considered the draft Report in its sitting held on 13 June, 2001 and adopted the same.  It also decided that the evidence tendered before the Committee may be laid on the Table of both the Houses of Parliament.
 
4.0       In course of its deliberations, the Committee has made use of the background note on the Bill and took note of recommendations of various Law Commissions, various judicial pronouncements on the subject, replies to queries raised by the Members on the Bill in the meetings, submitted by the Legislative Department and suggestions/observations made by experts/Church leaders.
 
5.         For facility of reference and convenience, observations and recommendations of the Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report.   
 
PRANAB MUKHERJEE
NEW DELHI
                                                                                                            Chairman
June 13, 2001.                                                                               Committee on Home Affairs
 
 
 
*  Published in the Gazette of India Extraordinary Part II, Section-2, dated 15 December, 2000.
**Rajya Sabha Parliamentary Bulletin Part II, No.38569 dated 19 December, 2000.
 
R
EPORT
 
1.0       The Indian Divorce (Amendment) Bill, 2000 (Annexure-I) seeks to amend  mainly sections 10, 17 and 20 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869* (see Annexure II)  suitably so as to remove the gender inequality as contained in section 10 and to do away with the procedural delays in  obtaining divorce due to the provisions contained in sections 17 and 20 of the Act.  The Bill also amends other sections of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869, which are consequential in nature.
 
1.1       It further proposes to repeal the following British Statutes relating to Christian Personal Law which have become obsolete but still in force, in pursuance of the recommendations of the Commission on Review of Administrative Laws set up under the Chairmanship of Shri P.C. Jain in 1998.
 
(i)
                  The Indian and Colonial Divorce Jurisdiction Act, 1926;
(ii)                The Indian and Colonial Divorce Jurisdiction Act, 1940; and
(iii)               The Indian Divorce Act, 1945.
 
2.0       Sections 10,17 and 20 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 are most contentious and have made the said Act evidently anachronistic, antiquated and discriminatory contrary to the provisions of the Constitution.
 
2.1.0    Section 10 of the Act provides for the grounds on which spouses belonging to Christian community can sue for dissolution of marriage.  In accordance with the provisions contained in the section, the male spouse is entitled to dissolution of marriage on the ground of adultery simpliciter   on the part  of female spouse but the female spouse  is not so entitled unless some other matrimonial fault is also found to be superadded to the adultery which are as below:-
 
(i)
                  incestuous  adultery;
(ii)                bigamy with  adultery;
(iii)               marriage with another women with adultery;
(iv)              adultery coupled with cruelty ; and
(v)                adultery coupled with desertion without reasonable excuse,  for two years or upwards.
 
Thus discrimination between the Christian female spouses   and Christian male spouses in seeking divorce is writ large as grounds available to female spouses are onerous and, thus, violative of constitutional provisions enshrined in Articles 14 and 15 of the Constitution.
 
2.1.1    Leaving aside adultery simpliciter, cruelty and desertion without reasonable excuse, for two years or upwards are not also independent grounds of seeking divorce a vinculo matrimonii  (a total divorce  releasing both the party wholly dissolving the marriage by releasing from their  matrimonial  obligations) for the female spouses.  However, the female spouse is entitled to  divorce  a mensa et thoro  ( a partial qualified divorce by which the parties are separated and forbidden to live or  cohabit  together without affecting the marriage itself)  under section 22 of the parent Act.  In other words, the grounds of adultery simpliciter, cruelty and desertion entails a Christian female spouse under the Act only judicial separation (i.e. separation from bed and board) not divorce proper.
______________________________________________________________________________
*This Act governs the divorce procedure  for the Christian community.
2.1.2    But the female spouses governed under other Acts governing divorce in India are entitled to divorce proper on the basis of the grounds mentioned in the foregoing para.  There are a total of five other Acts governing divorce in India which are as follows:-
 
(i)
                  The Hindu Marriage Act, 1955;
(ii)                The Dissolution of Muslim Marriage Act, 1939;
(iii)               The Parsi Marriage and Divorce Act, 1936;
(iv)              The Special Marriage Act, 1954; and
(v)                The Foreign Marriage Act, 1959.
 
2.1.3    It is relevant to note that Christian spouses can obtain divorce on the grounds specified  in para 2.1.1 supra  provided  that the marriage took place abroad in which case they are governed by the Foreign Marriage Act, 1959 (This Act applies to the Indian Citizen whose marriage has been solemnized in a foreign country).  Further, Christian couple married under the Special Marriage Act, 1954 are also entitled to get divorce on the grounds mentioned above.  Thus, the provision contained in section 22 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 discriminates Christian female spouses married in church vis-à-vis female spouses of other religions and Christian female spouse married under the Special Marriage Act and the Foreign Marriage Act and thus violative of Article 15 of the Constitution.
 
3.0       Sections 17 and 20 of the parent Act set out the confirmation procedure of decree of nullity of marriage of a District Court/High Court by a special bench of High Court which is conspicuous  by its absence in analogous Acts.  The special bench of High Court means bench consisting of three judges except where the total number of judges is two.  Parties who have obtained a decree nisi of dissolution of marriage from the District Court/High Court have to wait interminably till a special bench of three judges of High Court is constituted.  Thus, the requirement of compulsory confirmation of decree nisi of the District Court/High Court by the same High Court makes litigation protracted, prolonged and strenuous.  At present, the above sections are devoid of procedural   reasonableness
available to spouses belonging to other communities.
 
4.0       The provisions of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 are the exact copies of the English Matrimonial Causes Act, 1857.  The latter Act was enacted when the matrimonial jurisdiction was transferred from the Ecclesiastical Courts (Church Courts) to civil courts  (High Courts) and marriages were construed as dissoluble   contracts.  Limited grounds of dissolving marriages were provided under the Statute for the first time under English Law.  Prior to that, a divorce could only be obtained through an Act of Parliament.  The grounds mentioned in the English Act were incorporated under section 10 of the Indian Divorce Act , 1869 by the Britishers.
 
4.1       The English Matrimonial Causes Act, 1857 had been amended in England as early as 1923 by the Matrimonial Causes Act, 1923 putting the husband and the wife on equal footing by making adultery an independent ground of divorce for both the parties.  The Matrimonial Causes Act, 1937 added three new independent grounds of divorce i.e. desertion, cruelty and insanity and thereby became progressive.
 
4.2       The grounds provided in Matrimonial Causes Act, 1937 have been incorporated into analogous Acts governing divorce in India except Indian Divorce Act, 1869, Thus, the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 has not kept pace with the provisions of similar enactments relating to divorce in other communities which are far more progressive in character.  The Act even has not been alive to the amendments made in its parent Act i.e. Matrimonial Causes Act, 1857.  The Act appears to be antiquated and requires urgent amendment in order to make it progressive.
5.0       It is pertinent to note that Acts mentioned in paragraph 2.1.2 supra are unified legislations containing procedure relating to solemnization of marriage and divorce whereas Indian Divorce Act, 1869 contains provisions relating to divorce and matrimonial causes of persons professing Christian religion.  The procedures relating to the solemnization of marriage of persons of Christian community are governed by another legislation, namely, the Indian Christian Marriage Act, 1872 (see Annexure –III)
 
6.0       The Supreme Court and various High Courts especially Kerala, Andhra Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh, Bombay and Calcutta in their judicial pronouncements have held the view that the amendments to the sections 10, 17 and 20 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 are constitutionally   imperative.
 
6.1       The Law Commission of India in its Fifteenth, Twenty-second, Ninetieth and One Hundred and Sixty-fourth   Reports inter alia has held the similar view.
 
7.0       Marriage and divorce being in Concurrent List, the State of Uttar Pradesh has done away with the provisions in section 17 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 by a State Amendment, way back in 1957.
 
8.0       The Government of India realized the need for reforming the colonial laws relating to marriage and divorce of persons professing Christianity and, accordingly, took steps by making a reference to the Law Commission for revision of those laws in the year 1958.
 
8.1       The Commission prepared a unified legislation to repeal the existing Acts governing the subject of marriage and divorce amongst Christian community and circulated the same to State Governments and several churches and organizations of Christianity.  After taking into consideration the comments received and evidence taken by it, the Law Commission submitted its Fifteenth Report on “Laws relating to marriage and divorce amongst Christians in India” to Central Government in August, 1960, wherein a unified Bill, namely, the Christian Marriage and Matrimonial Causes Bill, 1961 was appended for introduction in Parliament.
 
8.2       The Government, however, at that time wanted the Law Commission to elicit public opinion on the Bill.  The Law Commission after eliciting public opinion submitted its Twenty-second Report in December 1961, wherein certain changes were made in the Bill referred to it.
 
8.3       Based on the recommendations contained in Twenty-second Report of the Law Commission,  the Christian Marriage and Matrimonial Causes Bill, 1962 was introduced in Lok Sabha, which was subsequently referred to a Joint Committee of both Houses.  Although the said Joint Committee had presented the Report to Parliament in 1963, the Bill could not come up  for discussion in Lok Sabha and lapsed on the dissolution of Third Lok Sabha.  The Government did not reintroduce the Bill due to strong  opposition from certain quarters of Christian community.
 
8.4       However,  in 1989, Shri Thampan Thomas, the then  Member of Parliament made an effort to bring in the legislation by introducing the Christian Marriage and Matrimonial Causes Bill, 1989 (Bill No.10 of 1989) in Lok Sabha on 10 March, 1989 as a Private Member’s Bill, but it could not come up for consideration.
 
8.5       The Law Commission headed by Mr. Justice K.K. Mathew suo motto took up the issue and in its Ninetieth Report on “Grounds of Divorce amongst Christians in India: section 10 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869”, submitted in May, 1983, had pointed out that section 10 of the Act is violative of Articles 14 and 15 of the Constitution and held the view that amendment to section 10 was a constitutional imperative.
 
8.6.0    The Law Commission again in its One Hundred and Sixty-fourth Report  on “The Indian Divorce Act, 1869”, presented in November, 1998, had reiterated  its stand taken in the Reports referred to  in foregoing paras.
 
8.6.1    It had inter alia recommended that Parliament may enact a comprehensive law governing marriage, divorce and other allied aspects of the Christians.  The Commission, while relying upon observations/judgments of various High Courts, had also urged the Government to take immediate measures to amend sections 10,17 and 20 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869.
 
8.7       The Committee on Status of Women in India in its Report submitted in 1974 had also suggested to amend the Act on the lines suggested by the Law Commission.
 
8.8       However, the Government did not find a congenial atmosphere in view of the situation that arose in view of the judgement in Supreme Court in Shah Banu’s case and in view of its avowed policy not to interfere in the personal laws of the minority community unless the necessary initiative for the changes came from the communities concerned.  However, the Government took up the cudgel when the Joint Women’s Programme (JWP), a voluntary women’s   social organization submitted the following Bills to the Government   and also claimed that the Bills had the support of various churches including Catholic Bishop’s Conference of India (CBCI):
 
(i)
                  The Christian Marriage  Bill, 1994;
(ii)                The Indian Sucession (Amendment) Bill, 1994; and
(iii)               The Christian Adoption and Maintenance Bill, 1994.
 
The Bill at Sr. No. (i) was a unified legislation substantially conforming to the provisions of the Christian Marriage and Matrimonial Causes Bill, 1962 and the Special Marriage Act, 1954.
 
8.9
              The National Commission for Minorities also agreed with the proposed legislation.
 
8.10     As mentioned in the Statement of Objects and Reasons of the Bill under examination of the Committee, a draft-unified legislation called the Christian Marriage Bill, 2000 (see Annexure- IV) was prepared.  A meeting was convened between the Government and prominent church leaders and the Members of Parliament belonging to Christian Community in April, 2000 but no consensus was arrived at on some provisions of the Bill.  However, there was no opposition for    the amendments to sections 10, 17 and 20 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869.
 
8.11     In view of the above factors, the Government has shown urgency to bring in the Indian Divorce (Amendment) Bill, 2000.  But the process which started way back in 1958 which is yet to be completed as there is a need to have a unified legislation on Personal Law of Christian Community as in the case of communities professing other religions in India.

REPORT
file:///D/Committee%20on%20Home%20Affairs/77threport.htm[30-06-2016 17:43:55]
 
9.0       The Secretary, Legislative Department in his deposition before the Committee gave a brief outline of the salient features of the Bill.  He stated that the Bill hovered around amendments to sections 10, 17 and 20 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 and the rest of the amendments were consequential in nature.  He stressed that minimal charges were to be effected in the parent Act without disturbing the scheme of the Act in order to bring gender equality and to do away with procedural delay in confirmation of decree of divorce.
 
9.1       He mentioned that the four grounds i.e.  ‘adultery’,  ‘conversion’, ‘cruelty’ and ‘desertion’ contained in section 10 of the Act, would now be available for divorce to both the sexes.  In addition, wife can also obtain divorce on the grounds of ‘rape’ and ‘sodomy’ on the part of husband.
 
9.2       He informed the Committee about the urgency of introducing the Bill in view of several judicial pronouncements and lack of consensus amongst Christian community on draft comprehensiveLegislation  prepared by the Government on marriage and divorce of Christian community.
 
10.0     The Committee, is of the view that the Bill is a half-baked measure and in that context, urges upon the Government to bring in a comprehensive legislation on  Personal Law of Christian community in consultation with that community at the earliest.                            
 
11.0     The Commission on Review of Administrative Laws constituted under the Chairmanship of Shri P.C.Jain had inter alia    stated that there were a number of personal laws applicable to some religions and communities and that some of these may not be relevant now.  The perception of the concerned religion of the community would be relevant for their updating/codification/ repeal.  The Commission had, inter alia identified Indian Christian Marriage Act, 1872 and the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 for the purpose.
 
12.0     Christians in India broadly belongs to three different traditions i.e. (i) Roman Catholic (ii) Protestants, who are followers of Church of South India (CSI) and Church of North India (CNI) and (iii) Syrian Christian, who are followers of Greek orthodox churches.  Christians amongst various tribes particularly in North-East Region are granted protection under the Constitution in respect of culture, traditions and customs.
 
12.1     Among the three traditions, the Roman Catholic Church considers marriage to be a sacrament and subscribes to the doctrine of indissolubility of marriage.  The other two Churches do not have doctrinaire objection to divorce.  However, in India the Christian community of different denominations do not have objection to divorce.  The Christian organizations, namely, Catholic Bishops’ Conference of India (CBCI) which represents nearly 50 per cent of Christian population and the National Council of Churches in India (NCCI) which is a conglomerate of 29 Protestant Churches representing 20-30 percent of Christian population have concurred with amendments in the Indian Divorce Act, 1869.  They have gone ahead in suggesting some additional amendments to the parent Act, which
was submitted to the Government on the day the Bill was presented to Parliament and the same has been placed as Annexure-V.
 
13.0.    The Committee also heard the representatives of Catholic Bishops’ Conference of India, National Council of Churches in India and Joint Women’s Programme collectively on the provisions of the Bill in its meeting held on 9 February, 2001.
 
 
13.1.0  The representatives of CBCI and NCCI stated that the teachings of the scriptures is ‘what God has united, let no man divide’ and, therefore, the Holy Scripture does not accept divorce.  However, in the larger interests of the community, and to free the parties from the civil bonds of marriage, they are accepting divorce and, therefore, appreciate the Bill as progressive. The witnesses felt that the provisions of section 10 of the Parent Act was inadequate and, therefore, in addition to what had already been provided in the Bill, the following other grounds, may be incorporated in the Bill;
 
(i)
                  incurability of an unsound mind for a continuous period of not less than two years immediately preceding the presentation of the petition;
(ii)                has been suffering from a virulent and incurable form of leprosy; for a period of not less than two years immediately preceding the presentation of the petition;
(iii)               has been suffering from venereal  disease in a communicable form for a period of not less than two years immediately preceding the presentation of the petition;
(iv)              has not been heard of being alive for a period of seven years or more; and 
(v)                willful refusal to consummate the marriage, and the marriage not having been consummated. 
 
13.1.1  They had also suggested addition of section 10 A to include ‘divorce by mutual consent’ to bring the Act at par with the Special Marriage Act, 1954 and the Hindu Marriage Act, 1955.
 
13.2     The representatives of the CBCI also stated that, Churches have certain procedures for declaring marriages null and void.  Force, fraud in getting the consent for marriage, insanity,  gross immaturity, etc. are  the grounds on the basis of which church nullify the marriage. They wanted that   such a declaration of marriage as null and void by the decisions of ecclesiastical  authorities functioning under the Church should be recognized  and ratified by the Civil Courts.
 
13.3     In that context, the Committee drew attention of the witnesses to section 19 of the Act which reads as under:
 
“Nothing in the Section shall affect the jurisdiction of the High Court to make decrees of nullity of marriage on the ground that the consent of either party was obtained by force.”
 
The Committee wanted to know the procedure followed by the Church in declaring the marriage null and void and requested the witnesses to clarify the position in case the decision of church comes into conflicts with decree of the court.
 
13.4     The representatives clarified that the set procedure for dissolution of marriage which is  followed all over the world is based on universal common law.  The procedure is similar to the provisions of the Civil Procedure Code by hearing petitions, respondants and examining witnesses.  The Church may say whether the parties can remarry but unless they get civil divorce, they cannot get married again.  Therefore, they suggested that instead of undertaking the whole procedure of a detailed examination by the Court, the Court can pronounce  its judgment after  examining their files maintained by the Church authorities.  Thus, the trial will be speedy and inexpensive and reduce emotional trauma.
 
13.5     The Committee desires that the aforesaid suggestion of the Church leaders may be examined by the Government.
 
13.6     Since Judicial precedents in India are sufficient, the witnesses had suggested to deletion of  section 7 of the Act  wherein the Court is free to act on the principle of English Divorce Court.  They had suggested for addition of a new definition of District Court under section 3 of the Act.
 
13.7     Regarding the provision which deals with damages from the adulterer such as sections 34 and 35 of the parent Act, the witnesses have suggested that these sections should be deleted in order to empower women and remove gender bias.
 
13.8     With regard to section 36 of the Act, providing one-fifth of the property   to be given to the divorced woman as maintenance, the witnesses were of the opinion that it should be dependent  on the status and the needs of the wife and the status of the husband and vice-versa. The witnesses also felt that in order to ensure gender justice, section 39, about the wife’s property being given for the benefit of the husband  and the children, be deleted.  As for sections 37, 43 and 44 dealing with alimony and custody of children, the witnesses strongly felt  that the woman was  the most important factor.
 
14.0     Suggestions received from these organizations are mostly in nature of amendments to various sections of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 not covered by the Bill.  Some of the suggestions would also require amendments to clauses 4, 13, 17, 18 and 19 of the Bill.  The comments of the Legislative Department on each suggestion have been received and are put at Annexure-VI   
 
15.0     The Committee took up clause-by-clause consideration of the Bill in its sitting held on 29 May 2001, in the presence of representatives of Legislative Department and   Department of Legal Affairs.
Clause 2
 
15.1.0  This clause seeks to amend the short title of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 ( as contained in section 1 of the Act ) so as to omit the word “Indian” occurring therein.  This is in tune with the recent legislative practice of not  using the word “Indian” in the short titles of Parliamentary legislation.
 
15.1.1  The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 3
 
15.1.2  It is proposed to remove gender inequality in the matter of grounds of divorce as available to husband and wife under section 10 of the Act by substituting that section vide clause 4 of the Bill.  As the expressions “incestuous adultery” and “bigamy with adultery” as occurring in section 10 of the Act will no longer be in the said section, these expressions have become redundant.  This clause, therefore, seeks to omit the definitions of those expressions occurring at clauses  (6) and (7) of section 3 of the Act.
 
15.1.3  The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 4
 
15.1.4  This clause seeks to provide the grounds of dissolution of marriage.  Existing provisions of section 10 of the Act make a distinction between the husband and the wife in the matter of grounds on which they could obtain dissolution of marriage.  While adultery, without any other   ground, is a ground for seeking dissolution  of marriage  by the husband, in the case of the petition by the wife, there is in addition another  requirement that  it should be ‘incestuous adultery’ or ‘bigamy with adultery’ or ‘adultery coupled with cruelty or desertion for two years’.  Certain High Courts, including the High Court of Kerala have struck down the words “incestuous” and “adultery coupled with” in section 10 on the ground of gender inequality.  This clause seeks to substitute the said section 10 with a
new section providing same grounds for husband and wife for seeking dissolution of marriage.   In addition, a wife may also get divorce  on the ground that the husband has since the solemnization of marriage been guilty of rape, sodomy or bestiality.
 
15.1.5  Under the Act a petition for dissolution of marriage could be presented either to the District Court or to the High Court.  It is proposed that the petition may henceforth be presented to the District Court only.  Hence, the clause amends section 10 of the parent Act.
 
15.1.6
    The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 5
 
15.1.7  This clause seeks to substitute existing section 11 with the addition of the words “or adulteress”  to cover cases where the wife sues the husband for divorce on the ground of adultery.  Thus both adulterer or adulteress would be co-respondent in case of divorce petition. The amendment is consequential to the amendments made in the grounds of divorce under section 10.
 
15.1.8  The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 6
 
15.1.9  This clause seeks to amend section  13 of the Act so as to do away with the enabling provision for presenting a petition for dissolution of marriage to the High Court after such a petition has been dismissed by the District  Court.
 
15.1.10            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 7
 
15.1.11            This clause seeks  to amend section 14 of the principal Act by omitting certain  words therein.  The amendment is consequential to the amendments made in sections 16 and 17 of the Act.
 
15.1.12            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 8
 
15.1.13This clause seeks to amend section 15 of the Act.  The amendments are consequential to the changes made in the grounds  of divorce under section 10.
 
15.1.14            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 9
 
15.1.15            This clause  seeks to amend  section 16 of the Act.  The amendment is consequential to the amendments made in section 17 of the Act to do away with the requirement of confirmation  of decree of District Court by the High Court.
 
15.1.16            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 10
 
15.1.17            This clause seeks to substitute section 17 relating to confirmation of decree  for dissolution  of marriages made by  a District Judge by the High Court.  At present  every  decree  for a   dissolution of marriage made by a  District Judge is subject to confirmation by the High Court.  In order  to obviate the procedural delay and  consequential hardship to the estranged couples, this clause seeks  to do away with the requirement of confirmation by the High Court of such  decrees. 
 
15.1.18            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 11
 
15.1.19            This clause seeks to omit section 17A of the Act relating to appointment of officers to exercise duties of King’s Proctor as it has become obsolete.
 
15.1.20            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 12
 
15.1.21            The clause seeks to amend section 18 of the Act relating to petition for decree of nullity.  The omission of the words  “or to the High Court” as proposed in this clause in consequential to the amendment proposed in section 10.
 
15.1.22            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 13
 
15.1.23            This clause seeks to amend section 19 of the Act as consequential to the amendment made in section 10 as regards the court in which petition may be presented.  Thus, it substitute   the words “the jurisdiction of the High Court” with the words the “jurisdiction of the District Court” occurring with last paragraph of the section.
 
15.1.24            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 14
 
15.1.25            This clause seeks to omit section 20 of the Act relating to confirmation of decree of nullity  of marriage by a  District Judge.  At present, every decree of nullity of marriage made by a District Judge shall be subject to confirmation by the High Court.  In order to simplify the procedure and obviate delay and consequential hardship to estranged  couples, it is proposed to do away with the requirement  of confirmation.
 
15.1.26            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 15
 
15.1.27            This clause seeks to amend section 22 of the Act.  The amendment is consequential to the changes made in section 10.
 
15.1.28            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
 
 
 
Clause 16
 
15.1.29            This clause seeks to omit the words “or the High Court” occurring in sections 23, 27 and 32 of the Act as consequential to changes made in section 10 relating to the court in which petitions could be made.
 
15.1.30            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 17
 
15.1.31            This clause provides for damages from adulterer or adulteress.  While following the existing provisions of section 34 of the Act, it is proposed to allow the wife also to claim damages from the adulteress.  This is consequential to the changes made in section 10 of the Act.
 
15.1.32            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 18
 
15.1.33            This clause seeks to substitute section 35 of the Act so as to make provision for the adulterer as well as the adulteress to pay costs.  The changes  are consequential to the changes made in section 10.
 
15.1.34            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clauses 19 to 23
 
15.1.35            The amendments suggested in these clauses are consequential to the changes made in sections 10 and 17 of the Act.
 
15.1.36            The Clauses were adopted without any change.
 
Clause 24
 
15.1.37            This clause seeks to amend section 45 of the Act relating to application of the Code of Civil Procedure by specifying the particulars of the present Code.
 
15.1.38            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 25
 
15.1.39            This clause seeks to amend section 52 of the Act as consequential to the changes made in section 10.
 
15.1.40            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
 
 
Clause 26
 
15.1.41            This clause seeks to amend section 55 of the Act so as to allow an appeal from a decree of a District Judge for dissolution of marriage or nullity of marriage.
 
15.1.42            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 27
 
15.1.43            This clause seeks to substitute section 57 of the Act specifying the circumstances under which the parties to a marriage which has been dissolved could marry again.
 
15.1.44            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 28
 
15.1.45            The amendment proposed in this clause is similar to the one proposed in clause 24.
 
15.1.46            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 29
 
15.1.47            This clause seeks to repeal certain British statutes which have become obsolete and recommended to be repealed by the Commission on Review of Administrative Laws.
 
15.1.48            The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause- 1, The Enacting Formula and Title
 
15.2     Clause 1, the Enacting Formula and the Title were adopted with some changes which are of consequential nature, namely, ‘2000’ and ‘Fifty-first’ to be substituted by ‘2001’ and ‘Fifty-second’, respectively.
 
15.3.0  The Committee recommends that the Bill be passed after incorporating amendments suggested in the Bill by Catholic Bishops’ Conference of India, National Council of Churches in India and Joint Women’s Programme to which the Government   have no objection.
 
15.3.1The Committee also recommends to the Government to consider the amendments suggested in those Sections of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869, not covered by the present Bill, received from Catholic Bishops’ Conference of India, National Council of Churches in India and Joint Women’s Programme on which the Government  have no objection, while piloting the Bill in the Parliament.
 
 
 
 
OBSERVATIONS/CONCLUSIONS/RECOMMENDATIONS OF THE COMMITTEE
 
 
 
The Committee, is of the view that the Bill is a half-baked measure and in that context, urges upon the Government to bring in a comprehensive legislation on  Personal Law of Christian community in consultation with that community at the earliest.                            
 
(Para 10.0)
 
·        The Committee desires that the aforesaid suggestion of the Church leaders may be examined by the Government.

WITNESSES

2.         The Chairman thereafter welcomed the witnesses and invited them to make a  joint presentation on the Indian Divorce (Amendment) Bill, 2000 and  give suggestions for the consideration of the Committee.  He also informed them that the deliberations of the meeting may be considered confidential until the Committee had submitted its report.  The Chairman also requested the witnesses to send their   written response to the points raised by Members be sent to the Secretariat within the next seven days.
 
2.1       The witnesses expressed their appreciation of the initiative taken for the much needed and progressive legislation by the Law Ministry.  They stated that the teachings of the scriptures did not permit divorce. However, in order to free a person from the civil effects of marriage, divorce by mutual consent may not be objected and be brought in as section 10A in the proposed Bill.
 
2.2       The witnesses also suggested that the civil courts should summarily be able to expedite marriages that the church had declared null and void after detailed enquiry and investigation, by recognizing and ratifying the decision.  With regard to definition of ‘District Court’ in Section 3, ‘within whose jurisdiction the marriage was solemnized or husband and wife reside or last resided’ may be added.
 
2.3     Sections 7, 34, 35 and 39 were suggested to be deleted.   Section 10 may be amended to bring it on par with the Special Marriage Act and the Hindu Marriage Act.
 
2.4       The witnesses suggested that alimony and maintenance in Section 36 should be based on the needs of the wife and the status of the parties.  Sections 37, 43 and 44 dealing with alimony and custody of children should be amended so as to bring about true gender equality.  The witness opined that, as for this matter, the woman was the most important factor.
 
3.         After the presentation of the witnesses, most of the Members expressed their endorsement of the suggestions proposed. Clarifications were sought on points like-whether a uniform civil code  for all individuals-Hindus, Muslims and Christians as in the Directive Principles would be accepted or whether abortion would be sufficient ground for seeking divorce.  A member wanted to know whether deletion of section 11 making an adulterer or the adulteress a respondent be accepted.  There was also a suggestion to substitute the words “the life of a prostitute” in clause 5 section 11 to “an immoral life”.  Another Member wanted to know how and when was a marriage declared null and void by a church.
 
(The witnesses then withdrew.)
 
4.         It was then decided that all the suggestions made be sent to the Government for their comments which would facilitate clause-by-clause consideration of the Bill by the Committee.
 
(The Committee adjourned at 12:30 P.M. and reassembled at 2:00 P.M.)

4.0       The Committee then took up clause-by-clause consideration of the Indian Divorce  (Amendment) Bill, 2000, in the presence of the officials of the Ministry of Law.
Clause 2
4.1.0    This clause seeks to amend the short title of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 ( as contained in section 1 of the Act ) so as to omit the word “Indian” occurring therein.  This is in tune with the recent legislative practice of not  using the word “Indian” in the short titles of Parliamentary legislation.
4.1.1    The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 3
4.1.2    It is proposed to remove gender inequality in the matter of grounds of divorce as available to husband and wife under section 10 of the Act by substituting that section vide clause   4 of   the  Bill.   As   the   expressions  “incestuous  adultery”  and  “bigamy  with
adultery” as occurring in section 10 of the Act will no longer be in the said section, these expressions have become redundant.  This clause, therefore, seeks to omit the definitions of those expressions occurring at clauses  (6) and (7) of section 3 of the Act.
4.1.3    The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 4
4.1.4    This clause seeks to provide the grounds of dissolution of marriage.  Existing provisions of section 10 of the Act make a distinction between the husband and the wife in the matter of grounds on which they could obtain dissolution of marriage.  While adultery, without any other   ground, is a ground for seeking dissolution  of marriage  by the husband, in the case of the petition by the wife, there is in addition another  requirement that  it should be ‘incestuous adultery’ or ‘bigamy with adultery’ or ‘adultery coupled with cruelty or desertion for two years’.  Certain High Courts, including the High Court of Kerala have struck down the words “incestuous” and “adultery coupled with” in section 10 on the ground of gender inequality.  This clause seeks to substitute the said section 10 with a
new section providing same grounds for husband and wife for seeking dissolution of marriage.   In addition, a wife may also get divorce  on the ground that the husband has since the solemnization of marriage been guilty of rape, sodomy or bestiality.
4.1.5    Under the Act a petition for dissolution of marriage could be presented either to the District Court or to the High Court.  It is proposed that the petition may henceforth be presented to the District Court only.  Hence, the clause amends section 10 of the parent Act.
4.1.6
        The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 5
4.1.7    This clause seeks to substitute existing section 11 with the addition of the words “or adulteress”  to cover cases where the wife sues the husband for divorce on the ground of adultery. 
Thus   both     adulterer    or    adulteress would be co-respondent in case of divorce petition. The
___________________________________________________________________________
*** Relates to other matters.
 
amendment is consequential to the amendments made in the grounds of divorce under section 10.
 
4.1.8    The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 6
4.1.9    This clause seeks to amend section 13 of the Act so as to do away with the enabling provision for presenting a petition for dissolution of marriage to the High Court after such a petition has been dismissed by the District  Court.
4.1.10  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 7
4.1.11  This clause seeks to amend section 14 of the principal Act by omitting certain words therein.  The amendment is consequential to the amendments made in sections 16 and 17 of the Act.
4.1.12  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 8
4.1.13  This clause seeks to amend section 15 of the Act.  The amendments are consequential to the changes made in the grounds of divorce under section 10.
4.1.14  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 9
4.1.15  This clause seeks to amend section 16 of the Act.  The amendment is consequential to the amendments made in section 17 of the Act to do away with the requirement of confirmation  of decree of District Court by the High Court.
4.1.16  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 10
4.1.17  This clause seeks to substitute section 17 relating to confirmation of decree  for dissolution  of marriages made by  a District Judge by the High Court.  At present every decree  for a   dissolution of marriage made by a  District Judge is subject to confirmation by the High Court.  In order to obviate the procedural delay and consequential hardship to the estranged couples, this clause seeks to do away with the requirement of confirmation by the High Court of such decrees. 
4.1.18  The Clause was adopted without any change.
 
Clause 11
4.1.19  This clause seeks to omit section 17A of the Act relating to appointment of officers to exercise duties of King’s Proctor as it has become obsolete.
4.1.20  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 12
4.1.21  The clause seeks to amend section 18 of the Act relating to petition for decree of nullity.  The omission of the words  “or to the High Court” as proposed in this clause in consequential to the amendment proposed in section 10.
4.1.22  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 13
4.1.23  This clause seeks to amend section 19 of the Act as consequential to the amendment made in section 10 as regards the court in which petition may be presented.  Thus, it substitute   the words “the jurisdiction of the High Court” with the words the “jurisdiction of the District Court” occurring with last paragraph of the section.
4.1.24  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 14
4.1.25  This clause seeks to omit section 20 of the Act relating to confirmation of decree of nullity  of marriage by a  District Judge.  At present, every decree of nullity of marriage made by a District Judge shall be subject to confirmation by the High Court.  In order to simplify the procedure and obviate delay and consequential hardship to estranged  couples, it is proposed to do away with the requirement  of confirmation.
4.1.26  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 15
4.1.27  This clause seeks to amend section 22 of the Act.  The amendment is consequential to the changes made in section 10.
4.1.28  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 16
4.1.29  This clause seeks to omit the words “or the High Court” occurring in sections 23, 27 and 32 of the Act as consequential to changes made in section 10 relating to the court in which petitions could be made.
4.1.30  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 17
4.1.31  This clause provides for damages from adulterer or adulteress.  While following the existing provisions of section 34 of the Act, it is proposed to allow the wife also to claim damages from the adulteress.  This is consequential to the changes made in section 10 of the Act.
4.1.32  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 18
4.1.33  This clause seeks to substitute section 35 of the Act so as to make provision for the adulterer as well as the adulteress to pay costs.  The changes  are consequential to the changes made in section 10.
4.1.34  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clauses 19 to 23
4.1.35  The amendments suggested in these clauses are consequential to the changes made in sections 10 and 17 of the Act.
4.1.36  The Clauses were adopted without any change.
Clause 24
4.1.37  This clause seeks to amend section 45 of the Act relating to application of the Code of Civil Procedure by specifying the particulars of the present Code.
4.1.38  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 25
4.1.39  This clause seeks to amend section 52 of the Act as consequential to the changes made in section 10.
4.1.40  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 26
4.1.41  This clause seeks to amend section 55 of the Act so as to allow an appeal from a decree of a District Judge for dissolution of marriage or nullity of marriage.
4.1.42  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 27
4.1.43  This clause seeks to substitute section 57 of the Act specifying the circumstances under which the parties to a marriage which has been dissolved could marry again.
4.1.44  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 28
4.1.45  The amendment proposed in this clause is similar to the one proposed in clause
4.1.46  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause 29
4.1.47  This clause seeks to repeal certain British statutes which have become obsolete and recommended to be repealed by the Commission on Review of Administrative Laws.
4.1.48  The Clause was adopted without any change.
Clause- 1, The Enacting Formula and Title
4.2       Clause 1, the Enacting Formula and the Title were adopted with some changes which are of consequential nature, namely, ‘2000’ and ‘Fifty-first’ to be substituted by ‘2001’ and ‘Fifty-second’, respectively.
4.3.0    The Committee was of the view that the Bill be passed after incorporating amendments suggested in the Bill by Catholic Bishops’ Conference of India, National Council of Churches in India and Joint Women’s Programme to which the Government   have no objection.
4.3.1   The Committee also felt that the Government should consider the amendments suggested in those Sections of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869, not covered by the present Bill, received from Catholic Bishops’ Conference of India, National Council of Churches in India and Joint Women’s Programme on which the Government have no objection, while piloting the Bill in the Parliament.

ANNEXURE

nexure I
AS INTRODUCED IN THE RAJYA SABHA

BILL NO.LXXVI OF 2000
THE INDIAN DIVORCE (AMENDMENT) BILL, 2000
A
BILL
further to amend the Indian Divorce Act, 1869.
 
BE it enacted by Parliament in the Fifty-first Year of the Republic of India as follows: ––
 
 
 
 
 
5
1.         (1) This Act may be called the Indian Divorce (Amendment) Act, 2000.
            (2) It shall come into force on such dates as the Central Government may, by notification in the Official Gazette, appoint.
Short title, and commencement
4 of 1869 2.     In section 1 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 (hereinafter referred to as the principal Act), the word “Indian” shall be omitted. Amendment of section 1.
 3.   In section 3 of the principal Act, clauses (6) and (7) shall be omitted. Amendment of section 3
Substitution of new section for section 10 4.  For section 10 of the principal Act, the following section shall be substituted, namely:––
 
     “10. (1) Any marriage solemnized whether before or after the commencement of the Indian Divorce )Amendment) Act, 2000 may, on a petition presented to the District Court either by the husband or the wife, be dissolved on the ground that since the solemnization of the marriage, the respondent ––
 
           i.          has been guilty of adultery;
          ii.          has changed her or his profession of Christianity for the profession of some other religion and gone  through a from of marriage with another man or woman, as the case may be;
        iii.          has treated the petitioner with such cruelty as to cause a reasonable apprehension in the mind of the petitioner that it would be harmful or injurious for the petitioner to live with the respondent. 
        iv.          has deserted the petitioner for at least two years immediately preceding the presentation of the petition.
 
(2)  A wife may also present a petition for the dissolution of her marriage by a decree of divorce on the ground that the husband has, since the solemnization of the marriage, been guilty of rape, sodomy or bestially.”.
 
 
 
5
 
 
 
 
 
 

REPORT
file:///D/Committee%20on%20Home%20Affairs/77threport.htm[30-06-2016 17:43:55]
 
 
 
 
 
10
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Substitution of new section for section 11.
 
Adulterer or adulteress to be co-respondent.
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
5.  For section 11 of the principal Act, the following section shall be substituted, namely:––
 
     “11. On a petition for dissolution of marriage presented by a husband or wife on the ground of adultery, the petitioner shall make the alleged adulterer or adulteress a co-respondent, unless the petitioner is excused by the Court from so doing on any of the following grounds, namely :––
 
a)
      that the wife, being the respondent is leading the life of a prostitute or the husband, being respondent is leading an immoral life and that the petitioner knows of no person with whom the adultery has been committed;
b)      that the name of the alleged adulterer or adulteress in unknown to the petitioner although the petitioner has made due efforts to discover it;
c)      that the alleged adulterer or adulteress is dead.”.
 
 
20
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
25
 
 
 
 
 
 
30
 
Amendment of section 13. 6.   In section 13 of the principal Act, the last paragraph shall be omitted.  
Amendment of section 14. 7.   In section 14 of the principal Act, in paragraph 4, the words “in the manner and subject to all the provisions and limitations in sections sixteen and seventeen made and declared” shall be omitted.  
Amendment of section 15. 8.  In section 15 of the principal Act, ––
(a)
    the words “without reasonable excuse” shall be omitted.
(b)    For the words “her adultery and cruelty”, the words her adultery, cruelty or desertion” shall be substituted.
(c)     for the words “such cruelty”, the words “such such adultery as cruelty” shall be substituted.
35
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
40
Amendment of section 16. 9.   In section 16 of the principal Act, the words “not being a confirmation of a decree of a district court” shall be omitted.  
 
 
 
 
5
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
10

     “17. STATEMENT OF OBJECTS AND REASONS
 
 
            The Law Commission of India in its 164th Reports on “The Indian Divorce Act (IV of 1869)” presented to the Government in November, 1998 has, inter alia, recommended that Parliament may enact a comprehensive law governing marriage and divorce and other allied aspects of the Christians in India.  The Commission, relying on the judgments and observations of certain High Courts, has also urged the Central Governments to take immediate measures––
(i)                 to amend section 10 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 relating to grounds of dissolution of marriages so that the female spouses are not discriminated vis a vis male spouse in obtaining a decree of dissolution of marriage.
(ii)               To amend suitably section 17 and 20 of the Act to do away with the procedural requirement of obtaining confirmation from the High Court in respect of a decree of dissolution of marriage or decree of nullity of marriage as such a procedure is long-drawn and strenuous.
2.         With a view to ascertaining the views of the Christian community on proposal for a unified law on marriage and divorce, the Central Government convened a meeting of leaders of prominent Churches in India and the Members of Parliament belonging to the Christian community on the 28th April, 2000 buy there was no consensus for bringing in a comprehensive legislation on Christian marriage and matrimonial causes.  However, there is no opposition from any one to amend sections 10. 17 and 20 of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 suitably to remove the gender inequality as contained in section 10 and to do away with the procedural delays in obtaining divorce due to the provisions contained in sections 17 and 20 of the Act.  The Government, therefore, proposes to make suitable changes
in the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 for removing hardship to all concerned.
3.         The Commission on Review of Administrative Laws which was set up by the Central Government on the 8th May, 1998 has, inter alia, recommended repeal of various enactment including three British Statutes relating to Christian Personal Law still in force.  It is proposed to repeal these enactments also they have become obsolete.
4.         This Bill seeks to achieve the above objects.
 
 
NEW DELHI;                                                                                      ARUN JAITLEY
The 17th November, 2000.
Notes on clauses
 
 
Clause 1. ––     This clause provides for short title and commencement of the Bill.
Clause 2. ––     This clause seeks to amend the short title of the Indian Divorce Act, 1869 (hereafter referred to as the Act) so as to omit the word “Indian” occurring therein.  This is in tune with the recent legislative practice of not using the word “Indian” in the short titles of Parliamentary legislation.
Clause 3. ––     It is proposed to remove gender inequality in the matter of grounds of divorce as available to husband and wife under section 10 of the Act by substituting that section vide clause 4 of the Bill.  As the expressions “incestuous adultery” and “bigamy with adultery” as occurring in section 10 of the Act will no longer be in the said section, these expressions have become redundant.  This clause, therefore, seeks to omit the definitions of those expressions.
Clause 4. ––     This clause seeks to provide the grounds of dissolution of marriage Existing provisions of section 10 of the Act make a distinction between the husband and the wife in the matter of grounds on which they could obtain dissolution of marriage.  While adultery, without any other ground, is a ground for seeking dissolution of marriage by the husband, in the case of the petition by the wife, there is in addition another requirement that it should be incestuous adultery or bigamy with adultery or adultery coupled with cruelty or desertion for two years.  Certain High Courts including the High Court of Kerala have struck down the words, “incestuous” and adultery coupled” with in section 10 on the ground of gender inequality.  This clause seeks to substitute the said section 10 with a new
section providing same grounds for husband and wife for seeking dissolution of marriage.  However, a wife may also get divorce on the ground that the husband has since the solemnization of marriage been guilty of rape, sodomy or bestiality.
            At present a petition for dissolution of marriage could be presented either to the District Court or to the High Court.  It is proposed that the petition may henceforth be presented to the District Court only.
Clause 5. ––     This clause seeks to substitute existing section 11 with the addition of the words “or adulteress” to cover cases where the wife sues the husband for divorce on the ground of adultery.  The amendment is consequential to the amendments made in the grounds of divorce under section 10.
Clause 6. ––     This clause seeks to amend section 13 of the Act so as to do away with the enabling provision for presenting a petition for dissolution of marriage to the High Court after such a petition has been dismissed by the District Court.
Clause 7. ––     This clause seeks to amend section 14 of the principal Act.  The amendment is consequential to the amendments made in sections 16 and 17 of the Act.
Clause 8. ––     This clause seeks to amend section 15 of the Act.  The amendments are consequential to the changes made in the grounds of divorce under section 10.
Clause 9. ––     This clause seeks to amend section 16 of the Act.  The amendment is consequential to the amendments made in section 17 of the Act to do away with the requirement of confirmation of decree of district Court by the High Court.
Clause 10. ––   This clause seeks to substitute section 17 relating to confirmation of decree for dissolution of marriages made by a District Judge by the High Court.  At present every decree for a dissolution of marriage made by a District Judge is subject to confirmation by the High Court.  In order to obviate the procedural delay and consequential hardship to the estranged couples, this clause seeks to do away with the requirement of confirmation by the High Court of such decrees.  The amendment is on the lines suggested by the Law Commission of India in its 164
th Report.
Clause 11. ––   This clause seeks to omit section 17A of the Act relating to appointment of officers to exercise, duties of King’s Proctor as it has become obsolete.
Clause 12. ––   This clause seeks to amend section 18 of the Act relating to petition for decree of nullity.  The omission of the words “or to the High Court” as proposed in this clause in consequential to the amendment proposed in section 10.
Clause 13. ––   This clause seeks to amend section 19 of the Act as consequential to the amendment made in section 10 as regards the court in which petition maybe presented.
Clause 14. ––   This clause seeks to omit section 20 of the Act relating to confirmation of decree of nullity of marriage by a District Judge.  At present, every decree of nullity of marriage made by a District Judge shall be subject to confirmation by the High Court.  In order to simplify the procedure and obviate delay and consequential hardship to estranged couples, it is proposed to do away with the requirement of confirmation.
Clause 15. ––   This clause seeks to amend section 22 of the Act.  The amendment is consequential to the changes made in section 10.
Clause 16. ––   This clause seeks to omit the words “or the High Court” occurring in sections 23, 27 and 32 of the Act as consequential to changes made in section 10 relating to the court in which petitions could be made.
Clause 17. ––   This clause provides for damages from adulterer or adulteress.  While following the existing provisions of section 34 of the Act, it is proposed to allow the wife also to claim damages from the adulteress.  This is consequential to the changes made in the section 10 of the Act.
Clause 18. ––   This clause seeks to substitute section 35 of the Act so as to make provision for the adulterer as well as the adulteress to pay costs.  The changes are consequential to the changes made in section 10.
Clause 19 to 23. ––      The amendments suggested in these clauses are consequential to the changes made in sections 10 and 17 of the Act.
Clause 24. ––   This clause seeks to amend section 45 of the Act relating to application of the Code of Civil Procedure by specifying the particulars of the present Code.
Clause 25. ––   This clause seeks to amend section 52 of the Act as consequential to the changes made in section 10.
Clause 26. ––   This clause seeks to amend section 55 of the Act so as to allow on appeal from  a decree of a District Judge for dissolution of marriage or nullity of marriage.
Clause 27. ––   This clause seeks to amend section 57 of the Act specifying the circumstances under which the parties to a marriage which has been dissolved could marry again.
Clause 28. ––   This amendment proposed in this clause is similar to the one proposed in clause 24.
Clause 29. ––   This clause seeks to repeal certain British statutes which have become obsolete and recommended to be repealed by the Commission on Review of Administrative Laws.

III –– DISSOLUTION OF MARRIAGE
 
 
 10.  Any husband may present a petition to the District Court or to the High Court, praying that his marriage may be dissolved on the ground that his wife has, since the solemnization thereof, been guilty of adultery.
      Any wife may present a petition to the District Court or to the High Court, praying that her marriage may be dissolved on the ground that, since the solemnization thereof, her husband has exchanged his profession of Christianity for the profession of some other religion, and gone through a form of marriage with another woman;
      or has been guilty of incestuous adultery.
      or of bigamy with adultery,
      or of marriage with another woman with

      or of rape, sodomy or bestiality,
      or of adultery coupled with such cruelty as without would have entitled her to a divorce a mena et toro.
      or of adultery coupled with desertion, without reasonable excuse, for two years or upwards.
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
       Every such petition shall state, as distinctly as the nature of the case permits, the facts on which the claim to have such marriage dissolved is founded. Contents of petition.
 
 11.  Upon any such petition presented by a husband, the petitioner shall make the alleged adulterer a co-respondent to the said petition, unless he is excused from so doing on one of the following grounds, to be allowed by the Court:–
(1)
    that the respondent is leading the life of a prostitute, and that the petitioner knows of no person with whom the adultery has been committed;
(2)    that the name of the alleged adulterer is unknown to the petitioner, although he has made due efforts to discover it;
(3)    that the alleged adulterer is dead.
Adulterer to be co-respondent

Dismissal of petition 13.  In case the Court, on the evidence in relation to any such petition, is satisfied that the petitioners case has not been proved, or is not satisfied that the alleged adultery has been committed.
     or finds that he petitioner has, during the marriage, or the adultery of the other party to the marriage, or has condoned the adultery complained of,
     or that the petition is presented or prosecuted in collusion with either of the respondents,
    then and in any of the said cases the Court shall dismiss the petition.
    when a petition is dismissed by a District Court under this section, the petitioner may, nevertheless, present a similar petition to the High Court.
 
Power to Court to pronounce decree for dissolving marriage.

     and does not find that the petitioner has been in any manner accessory to, or conniving at, the going through of the said form of marriage, or the adultery of the other party to the marriage, or has condoned the adultery complained of,
